Well, doctor, I... I came in because the ladies at church said I should. I keep doing odd things lately, I guess. Like at the Sunday potluck, I was just standing by the long table holding the big serving spoon straight up in the air and not moving. Just frozen there. That spoon is real heavy, you know, it belonged to Pastor Miller’s mother before she passed. She used to grow the biggest tomatoes you ever saw in her backyard, right up against the fence. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the little green bugs got to 'em before they even turned red. 

But anyway, they said people were waiting on me to serve the green beans, and I was just standing there holding it. Or another time, I was folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks were waiting on me to hand them out. I can't quite recall. The cake was lemon, though.

Speaking less? Well, I don't rightly know. I suppose I still talk to the ladies at church. And I talk to my cat, Barnaby. He's a big orange tabby, you know. Or another time, I was folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks were waiting on me to hand them out. They were those nice yellow paper napkins, kind of like the ones we had at my sister's 50th anniversary party... she wore a beautiful blue dress that day, or maybe it was purple... I can't quite recall. The cake was lemon, though. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, right. So I'm here because I just keep doing those strange things at the church.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or irritable for several days?\n\nPatient: Energized? Oh, no, not me. I don't have much energy at all anymore. I usually just sit and drink my coffee and watch the birds. We get these little blue jays in the yard. They sure are loud, though. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he put out a bird feeder last spring... it was one of those wooden ones shaped like a little barn. He painted it red and everything. But the squirrels got into it right away. They're so clever, those squirrels, they just hang upside down by their little toes and eat all the sunflower seeds. He tried putting grease on the metal pole so they'd slide off, but it didn't even stop 'em. I told him he ought to just feed the squirrels, they gotta eat too, you know? They have those big bushy tails... I used to have a coat with a fur collar, kind of looked like a squirrel tail, but it was rabbit, I think. I wore it to church every winter until the lining ripped out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been speaking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking less? Well, I don't rightly know. I suppose I still talk to the ladies at church. And I talk to my cat, Barnaby. He's a big orange tabby, you know. Found him under the porch about five years ago... or maybe it was six. It was raining so hard that day, the street was practically a river. My basement flooded just a little bit, right by the washing machine. I had to put down all those old towels to soak it up. They were the green ones I bought at that discount store that closed down... the one next to the bakery. Oh, they had the best apple fritters there. I used to get one every Tuesday. But anyway, Barnaby just sat there on the porch step looking so pitiful, I had to bring him in. He really likes those little cans of tuna, but you can't give them too much or they get an upset stomach. So, I don't know if I speak less... I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 71 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.

Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. Well, except maybe the radio. I keep a little radio on the kitchen counter, just for some company, you know? It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.

Like at the Sunday potluck, I was just standing by the long table holding the big serving spoon straight up in the air and not moving. Just frozen there. That spoon is real heavy, you know, it belonged to Pastor Miller’s mother before she passed. She used to grow the biggest tomatoes you ever saw in her backyard, right up against the fence. So I'm here because I just keep doing those strange things at the church.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or irritable for several days?\n\nPatient: Energized? My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he put out a bird feeder last spring... it was one of those wooden ones shaped like a little barn. He painted it red and everything. But the squirrels got into it right away. They're so clever, those squirrels, they just hang upside down by their little toes and eat all the sunflower seeds. He tried putting grease on the metal pole so they'd slide off, but it didn't even stop 'em. I told him he ought to just feed the squirrels, they gotta eat too, you know? They have those big bushy tails... I used to have a coat with a fur collar, kind of looked like a squirrel tail, but it was rabbit, I think. I wore it to church every winter until the lining ripped out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been speaking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking less? Well, I don't rightly know. I suppose I still talk to the ladies at church. And I talk to my cat, Barnaby. He's a big orange tabby, you know. Found him under the porch about five years ago... or maybe it was six. It was raining so hard that day, the street was practically a river. My basement flooded just a little bit, right by the washing machine. I had to put down all those old towels to soak it up. They were the green ones I bought at that discount store that closed down... the one next to the bakery. Oh, they had the best apple fritters there. I used to get one every Tuesday. But anyway, Barnaby just sat there on the porch step looking so pitiful, I had to bring him in. He really likes those little cans of tuna, but you can't give them too much or they get an upset stomach. So, I don't know if I speak less... I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Stiff? Well, I couldn't rightly say. Nobody's ever tried to move me when I get like that. My niece, Sarah, she came over once when I was just sitting in the kitchen. She brought me a lovely pot of African violets. You have to be so careful watering those, you know. If you get water on the leaves, they get all spotty and brown. I keep them on the windowsill above the sink so they get just enough light but not too much direct sun. The afternoon sun in that window can be terribly strong. It faded the curtains I made... I sewed them myself on my old Singer machine. The pedal on that machine sticks sometimes if you press too hard, and then it just runs away with you. I ruined a whole hem on a skirt once because of that pedal. It was a lovely navy blue wool skirt, too. Perfect for church in the winter. But anyway, Sarah just sat and waited for me to come back around. She didn't try to move my arm or anything, so I just don't know if I'm stiff or not.

They were the green ones I bought at that discount store that closed down... the one next to the bakery. Oh, they had the best apple fritters there. I used to get one every Tuesday. But anyway, Barnaby just sat there on the porch step looking so pitiful, I had to bring him in. He really likes those little cans of tuna, but you can't give them too much or they get an upset stomach. So, I don't know if I speak less... I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. Well, except maybe the radio. I keep a little radio on the kitchen counter, just for some company, you know? It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, if someone tries to move your arm or hand, does it feel stiff or hard to move?\n\nPatient: Stiff? Well, I couldn't rightly say. Nobody's ever tried to move me when I get like that. My niece, Sarah, she came over once when I was just sitting in the kitchen. She brought me a lovely pot of African violets. You have to be so careful watering those, you know. If you get water on the leaves, they get all spotty and brown. I keep them on the windowsill above the sink so they get just enough light but not too much direct sun. The afternoon sun in that window can be terribly strong. It faded the curtains I made... I sewed them myself on my old Singer machine. The pedal on that machine sticks sometimes if you press too hard, and then it just runs away with you. I ruined a whole hem on a skirt once because of that pedal. It was a lovely navy blue wool skirt, too. Perfect for church in the winter. But anyway, Sarah just sat and waited for me to come back around. She didn't try to move my arm or anything, so I just don't know if I'm stiff or not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sad? Oh, no, I don't think so. I try to keep a smile on, you know? There's always something to be cheerful about if you look for it. Like my bird feeder out back. I put it up just so I could watch the cardinals in the morning while I have my tea. They are such a beautiful bright red. But those squirrels, I tell you, they are just absolute menaces! They climb right up the pole and steal all the expensive black oil sunflower seeds. 

My neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he lives in the blue house next door, the one with the peeling paint on the porch railings—he told me to rub Crisco all over the metal pole so the squirrels would just slide right down. Well, I tried it, but it just made a terrible greasy mess of my hands and the patio. I usually only use Crisco when I'm baking pies. You have to make sure the shortening is real cold, and you only use ice water, otherwise the crust won't be flaky at all. My grandmother taught me that when I was just a little girl. She used to make the most wonderful strawberry rhubarb pies from the patch behind her barn. The rhubarb stalks were as thick as your wrist! We'd eat it warm on the back porch with a big scoop of vanilla ice cream melting all over the top... 

But, oh, what were you asking? Depressed? No, no, I don't get down in the dumps for days like that. I just keep busy with my little things.

I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. Well, except maybe the radio. I keep a little radio on the kitchen counter, just for some company, you know? It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, if someone tries to move your arm or hand, does it feel stiff or hard to move?\n\nPatient: Stiff? Well, I couldn't rightly say. Nobody's ever tried to move me when I get like that. My niece, Sarah, she came over once when I was just sitting in the kitchen. She brought me a lovely pot of African violets. You have to be so careful watering those, you know. If you get water on the leaves, they get all spotty and brown. I keep them on the windowsill above the sink so they get just enough light but not too much direct sun. The afternoon sun in that window can be terribly strong. It faded the curtains I made... I sewed them myself on my old Singer machine. The pedal on that machine sticks sometimes if you press too hard, and then it just runs away with you. I ruined a whole hem on a skirt once because of that pedal. It was a lovely navy blue wool skirt, too. Perfect for church in the winter. But anyway, Sarah just sat and waited for me to come back around. She didn't try to move my arm or anything, so I just don't know if I'm stiff or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Sad? Oh, no, I don't think so. I try to keep a smile on, you know? There's always something to be cheerful about if you look for it. Like my bird feeder out back. I put it up just so I could watch the cardinals in the morning while I have my tea. They are such a beautiful bright red. But those squirrels, I tell you, they are just absolute menaces! They climb right up the pole and steal all the expensive black oil sunflower seeds. \n\nMy neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he lives in the blue house next door, the one with the peeling paint on the porch railings—he told me to rub Crisco all over the metal pole so the squirrels would just slide right down. Well, I tried it, but it just made a terrible greasy mess of my hands and the patio. I usually only use Crisco when I'm baking pies. You have to make sure the shortening is real cold, and you only use ice water, otherwise the crust won't be flaky at all. My grandmother taught me that when I was just a little girl. She used to make the most wonderful strawberry rhubarb pies from the patch behind her barn. The rhubarb stalks were as thick as your wrist! We'd eat it warm on the back porch with a big scoop of vanilla ice cream melting all over the top... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Depressed? No, no, I don't get down in the dumps for days like that. I just keep busy with my little things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Posture? Oh, I couldn't really say for sure. I suppose I just stay however I was already sitting or standing. I don't think I'm holding a pose on purpose or anything. 

Speaking of posture, my mother used to always tap me between the shoulder blades and say, "Shoulders back, young lady!" She was a real stickler for sitting up straight. And my teacher in the fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was exactly the same. We had those heavy old wooden desks at school, the kind where your seat was bolted right to the desk behind you. They even had the little round holes cut out for glass inkwells, though we only ever really used pencils. My father bought me a whole pack of yellow pencils from the five-and-dime once, and he'd sharpen every single one of them with his pocket knife. He always kept that little knife so sharp... he'd sit on the front porch in the evenings and whittle little boats out of pine scraps for me and my brother. We'd take them right down to the creek behind the house and watch the current take them away. That water was always so cold, even in the middle of July. We used to catch tadpoles in old glass mayonnaise jars... 

But, oh, what were we talking about? Freezing? No, I don't know that I hold any special posture, I just sort of pause for a bit.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF06.1, F20.2, F20.9, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I came in because the ladies at church said I should. I keep doing odd things lately, I guess. Like at the Sunday potluck, I was just standing by the long table holding the big serving spoon straight up in the air and not moving. Just frozen there. That spoon is real heavy, you know, it belonged to Pastor Miller’s mother before she passed. She used to grow the biggest tomatoes you ever saw in her backyard, right up against the fence. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the little green bugs got to 'em before they even turned red. \n\nBut anyway, they said people were waiting on me to serve the green beans, and I was just standing there holding it. Or another time, I was folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks were waiting on me to hand them out. They were those nice yellow paper napkins, kind of like the ones we had at my sister's 50th anniversary party... she wore a beautiful blue dress that day, or maybe it was purple... I can't quite recall. The cake was lemon, though. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, right. So I'm here because I just keep doing those strange things at the church.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or irritable for several days?\n\nPatient: Energized? Oh, no, not me. I don't have much energy at all anymore. I usually just sit and drink my coffee and watch the birds. We get these little blue jays in the yard. They sure are loud, though. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he put out a bird feeder last spring... it was one of those wooden ones shaped like a little barn. He painted it red and everything. But the squirrels got into it right away. They're so clever, those squirrels, they just hang upside down by their little toes and eat all the sunflower seeds. He tried putting grease on the metal pole so they'd slide off, but it didn't even stop 'em. I told him he ought to just feed the squirrels, they gotta eat too, you know? They have those big bushy tails... I used to have a coat with a fur collar, kind of looked like a squirrel tail, but it was rabbit, I think. I wore it to church every winter until the lining ripped out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been speaking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking less? Well, I don't rightly know. I suppose I still talk to the ladies at church. And I talk to my cat, Barnaby. He's a big orange tabby, you know. Found him under the porch about five years ago... or maybe it was six. It was raining so hard that day, the street was practically a river. My basement flooded just a little bit, right by the washing machine. I had to put down all those old towels to soak it up. They were the green ones I bought at that discount store that closed down... the one next to the bakery. Oh, they had the best apple fritters there. I used to get one every Tuesday. But anyway, Barnaby just sat there on the porch step looking so pitiful, I had to bring him in. He really likes those little cans of tuna, but you can't give them too much or they get an upset stomach. So, I don't know if I speak less... I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. Well, except maybe the radio. I keep a little radio on the kitchen counter, just for some company, you know? It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, if someone tries to move your arm or hand, does it feel stiff or hard to move?\n\nPatient: Stiff? Well, I couldn't rightly say. Nobody's ever tried to move me when I get like that. My niece, Sarah, she came over once when I was just sitting in the kitchen. She brought me a lovely pot of African violets. You have to be so careful watering those, you know. If you get water on the leaves, they get all spotty and brown. I keep them on the windowsill above the sink so they get just enough light but not too much direct sun. The afternoon sun in that window can be terribly strong. It faded the curtains I made... I sewed them myself on my old Singer machine. The pedal on that machine sticks sometimes if you press too hard, and then it just runs away with you. I ruined a whole hem on a skirt once because of that pedal. It was a lovely navy blue wool skirt, too. Perfect for church in the winter. But anyway, Sarah just sat and waited for me to come back around. She didn't try to move my arm or anything, so I just don't know if I'm stiff or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Sad? Oh, no, I don't think so. I try to keep a smile on, you know? There's always something to be cheerful about if you look for it. Like my bird feeder out back. I put it up just so I could watch the cardinals in the morning while I have my tea. They are such a beautiful bright red. But those squirrels, I tell you, they are just absolute menaces! They climb right up the pole and steal all the expensive black oil sunflower seeds. \n\nMy neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he lives in the blue house next door, the one with the peeling paint on the porch railings—he told me to rub Crisco all over the metal pole so the squirrels would just slide right down. Well, I tried it, but it just made a terrible greasy mess of my hands and the patio. I usually only use Crisco when I'm baking pies. You have to make sure the shortening is real cold, and you only use ice water, otherwise the crust won't be flaky at all. My grandmother taught me that when I was just a little girl. She used to make the most wonderful strawberry rhubarb pies from the patch behind her barn. The rhubarb stalks were as thick as your wrist! We'd eat it warm on the back porch with a big scoop of vanilla ice cream melting all over the top... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Depressed? No, no, I don't get down in the dumps for days like that. I just keep busy with my little things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you keep the same posture for a while without moving at all?\n\nPatient: Posture? Oh, I couldn't really say for sure. I suppose I just stay however I was already sitting or standing. I don't think I'm holding a pose on purpose or anything. \n\nSpeaking of posture, my mother used to always tap me between the shoulder blades and say, \"Shoulders back, young lady!\" She was a real stickler for sitting up straight. And my teacher in the fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was exactly the same. We had those heavy old wooden desks at school, the kind where your seat was bolted right to the desk behind you. They even had the little round holes cut out for glass inkwells, though we only ever really used pencils. My father bought me a whole pack of yellow pencils from the five-and-dime once, and he'd sharpen every single one of them with his pocket knife. He always kept that little knife so sharp... he'd sit on the front porch in the evenings and whittle little boats out of pine scraps for me and my brother. We'd take them right down to the creek behind the house and watch the current take them away. That water was always so cold, even in the middle of July. We used to catch tadpoles in old glass mayonnaise jars... \n\nBut, oh, what were we talking about? Freezing? No, I don't know that I hold any special posture, I just sort of pause for a bit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Oh, no, I don't think so. I try to keep a smile on, you know? There's always something to be cheerful about if you look for it. Like my bird feeder out back. I put it up just so I could watch the cardinals in the morning while I have my tea. They are such a beautiful bright red. But those squirrels, I tell you, they are just absolute menaces! They climb right up the pole and steal all the expensive black oil sunflower seeds. \n\nMy neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he lives in the blue house next door, the one with the peeling paint on the porch railings—he told me to rub Crisco all over the metal pole so the squirrels would just slide right down. Well, I tried it, but it just made a terrible greasy mess of my hands and the patio. I usually only use Crisco when I'm baking pies. You have to make sure the shortening is real cold, and you only use ice water, otherwise the crust won't be flaky at all. My grandmother taught me that when I was just a little girl. She used to make the most wonderful strawberry rhubarb pies from the patch behind her barn. The rhubarb stalks were as thick as your wrist! We'd eat it warm on the back porch with a big scoop of vanilla ice cream melting all over the top... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Depressed? No, no, I don't get down in the dumps for days like that. I just keep busy with my little things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you keep the same posture for a while without moving at all?\n\nPatient: Posture? Oh, I couldn't really say for sure. I suppose I just stay however I was already sitting or standing. I don't think I'm holding a pose on purpose or anything. \n\nSpeaking of posture, my mother used to always tap me between the shoulder blades and say, \"Shoulders back, young lady!\" She was a real stickler for sitting up straight. And my teacher in the fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was exactly the same. We had those heavy old wooden desks at school, the kind where your seat was bolted right to the desk behind you. They even had the little round holes cut out for glass inkwells, though we only ever really used pencils. My father bought me a whole pack of yellow pencils from the five-and-dime once, and he'd sharpen every single one of them with his pocket knife. He always kept that little knife so sharp... he'd sit on the front porch in the evenings and whittle little boats out of pine scraps for me and my brother. We'd take them right down to the creek behind the house and watch the current take them away. That water was always so cold, even in the middle of July. We used to catch tadpoles in old glass mayonnaise jars... \n\nBut, oh, what were we talking about? Freezing? No, I don't know that I hold any special posture, I just sort of pause for a bit.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that your face or voice has become less expressive or more flat than it used to be?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 71 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
My face? Oh, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I've always had a quiet sort of voice, I suppose. My husband, God rest his soul, he used to say I was as quiet as a church mouse when we first met. We met at a church social, actually. They had the most wonderful potluck dinners back then. Everyone would bring something in their best Pyrex dish. I always brought my famous green bean casserole with those crispy little fried onions on top. You have to make sure you bake it just long enough so the onions get golden brown, but not burnt. I burnt them once, right before Thanksgiving, and the whole house smelled like smoke for three days. We had to open all the windows, and it was freezing out! The wind was just howling through the trees in the front yard. We had this big old oak tree that would drop acorns all over the driveway, and it sounded like hail hitting the roof of the car...

But, no, I don't think my face or voice has changed. I'm just the same as I've always been.

I can't quite recall. The cake was lemon, though. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, right. So I'm here because I just keep doing those strange things at the church.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or irritable for several days?\n\nPatient: Energized? Oh, no, not me. I don't have much energy at all anymore. I usually just sit and drink my coffee and watch the birds. We get these little blue jays in the yard. They sure are loud, though. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he put out a bird feeder last spring... it was one of those wooden ones shaped like a little barn. He painted it red and everything. But the squirrels got into it right away. They're so clever, those squirrels, they just hang upside down by their little toes and eat all the sunflower seeds. He tried putting grease on the metal pole so they'd slide off, but it didn't even stop 'em. I told him he ought to just feed the squirrels, they gotta eat too, you know? They have those big bushy tails... I used to have a coat with a fur collar, kind of looked like a squirrel tail, but it was rabbit, I think. I wore it to church every winter until the lining ripped out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been speaking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking less? Well, I don't rightly know. I suppose I still talk to the ladies at church. And I talk to my cat, Barnaby. He's a big orange tabby, you know. Found him under the porch about five years ago... or maybe it was six. It was raining so hard that day, the street was practically a river. My basement flooded just a little bit, right by the washing machine. I had to put down all those old towels to soak it up. They were the green ones I bought at that discount store that closed down... the one next to the bakery. Oh, they had the best apple fritters there. I used to get one every Tuesday. But anyway, Barnaby just sat there on the porch step looking so pitiful, I had to bring him in. He really likes those little cans of tuna, but you can't give them too much or they get an upset stomach. So, I don't know if I speak less... I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. Well, except maybe the radio. I keep a little radio on the kitchen counter, just for some company, you know? It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, if someone tries to move your arm or hand, does it feel stiff or hard to move?\n\nPatient: Stiff? Well, I couldn't rightly say. Nobody's ever tried to move me when I get like that. My niece, Sarah, she came over once when I was just sitting in the kitchen. She brought me a lovely pot of African violets. You have to be so careful watering those, you know. If you get water on the leaves, they get all spotty and brown. I keep them on the windowsill above the sink so they get just enough light but not too much direct sun. The afternoon sun in that window can be terribly strong. It faded the curtains I made... I sewed them myself on my old Singer machine. The pedal on that machine sticks sometimes if you press too hard, and then it just runs away with you. I ruined a whole hem on a skirt once because of that pedal. It was a lovely navy blue wool skirt, too. Perfect for church in the winter. But anyway, Sarah just sat and waited for me to come back around. Oh, no, I don't think so. I try to keep a smile on, you know? There's always something to be cheerful about if you look for it. Like my bird feeder out back. I put it up just so I could watch the cardinals in the morning while I have my tea. They are such a beautiful bright red. But those squirrels, I tell you, they are just absolute menaces! They climb right up the pole and steal all the expensive black oil sunflower seeds. \n\nMy neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he lives in the blue house next door, the one with the peeling paint on the porch railings—he told me to rub Crisco all over the metal pole so the squirrels would just slide right down. Well, I tried it, but it just made a terrible greasy mess of my hands and the patio. I usually only use Crisco when I'm baking pies. She used to make the most wonderful strawberry rhubarb pies from the patch behind her barn. The rhubarb stalks were as thick as your wrist! We'd eat it warm on the back porch with a big scoop of vanilla ice cream melting all over the top... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Depressed? No, no, I don't get down in the dumps for days like that. I suppose I just stay however I was already sitting or standing. I don't think I'm holding a pose on purpose or anything. \n\nSpeaking of posture, my mother used to always tap me between the shoulder blades and say, \"Shoulders back, young lady!\" She was a real stickler for sitting up straight. And my teacher in the fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was exactly the same. We had those heavy old wooden desks at school, the kind where your seat was bolted right to the desk behind you. They even had the little round holes cut out for glass inkwells, though we only ever really used pencils. My father bought me a whole pack of yellow pencils from the five-and-dime once, and he'd sharpen every single one of them with his pocket knife. He always kept that little knife so sharp... he'd sit on the front porch in the evenings and whittle little boats out of pine scraps for me and my brother. We'd take them right down to the creek behind the house and watch the current take them away. That water was always so cold, even in the middle of July. We used to catch tadpoles in old glass mayonnaise jars... \n\nBut, oh, what were we talking about? Freezing? No, I don't know that I hold any special posture, I just sort of pause for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your face or voice has become less expressive or more flat than it used to be?\n\nPatient: My face? Oh, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I've always had a quiet sort of voice, I suppose. My husband, God rest his soul, he used to say I was as quiet as a church mouse when we first met. We met at a church social, actually. They had the most wonderful potluck dinners back then. Everyone would bring something in their best Pyrex dish. I always brought my famous green bean casserole with those crispy little fried onions on top. You have to make sure you bake it just long enough so the onions get golden brown, but not burnt. I burnt them once, right before Thanksgiving, and the whole house smelled like smoke for three days. We had to open all the windows, and it was freezing out! The wind was just howling through the trees in the front yard. We had this big old oak tree that would drop acorns all over the driveway, and it sounded like hail hitting the roof of the car...\n\nBut, no, I don't think my face or voice has changed. I'm just the same as I've always been.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt much more energetic or excited than usual, along with unusual thoughts or behavior?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Energetic? Oh, goodness, no. I don't have that kind of energy anymore. I just take my time with things nowadays. I do like a good cup of coffee in the morning to get me going, though. I still use my old stovetop percolator. It makes that wonderful bubbling sound on the burner. I bought it at Miller's Hardware downtown... oh, it must have been forty years ago. Mr. Miller was such a sweet man. He always wore these heavy denim overalls and he kept a big wooden barrel of peppermint sticks right by the cash register. My brother used to suck on those peppermints until they were sharp as a needle, and then he'd poke me in the arm with it on the walk home. We had to walk two miles to town, right along the old dirt road, and the dust would just coat your shoes by the time you got there. My mother would make us take them off on the porch and beat them against the steps so we wouldn't track dirt into the kitchen. She was so particular about her floors. She used to scrub the linoleum on her hands and knees with a bucket of hot soapy water and a bristle brush... 

But, oh, I am sorry, what were you asking? Excited? No, no, I don't get all worked up or energetic for days on end. Just slow and steady for me.

I tried growing tomatoes once, but the little green bugs got to 'em before they even turned red. \n\nBut anyway, they said people were waiting on me to serve the green beans, and I was just standing there holding it. Or another time, I was folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks were waiting on me to hand them out. I can't quite recall. The cake was lemon, though. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, right. So I'm here because I just keep doing those strange things at the church.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or irritable for several days?\n\nPatient: Energized? Oh, no, not me. I don't have much energy at all anymore. I usually just sit and drink my coffee and watch the birds. We get these little blue jays in the yard. They sure are loud, though. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he put out a bird feeder last spring... it was one of those wooden ones shaped like a little barn. He painted it red and everything. But the squirrels got into it right away. They're so clever, those squirrels, they just hang upside down by their little toes and eat all the sunflower seeds. He tried putting grease on the metal pole so they'd slide off, but it didn't even stop 'em. I told him he ought to just feed the squirrels, they gotta eat too, you know? They have those big bushy tails... I used to have a coat with a fur collar, kind of looked like a squirrel tail, but it was rabbit, I think. I suppose I still talk to the ladies at church. And I talk to my cat, Barnaby. He's a big orange tabby, you know. Found him under the porch about five years ago... or maybe it was six. It was raining so hard that day, the street was practically a river. My basement flooded just a little bit, right by the washing machine. I had to put down all those old towels to soak it up. They were the green ones I bought at that discount store that closed down... the one next to the bakery. Oh, they had the best apple fritters there. I used to get one every Tuesday. But anyway, Barnaby just sat there on the porch step looking so pitiful, I had to bring him in. He really likes those little cans of tuna, but you can't give them too much or they get an upset stomach. So, I don't know if I speak less... I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. Well, except maybe the radio. I keep a little radio on the kitchen counter, just for some company, you know? It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, if someone tries to move your arm or hand, does it feel stiff or hard to move?\n\nPatient: Stiff? Well, I couldn't rightly say. Nobody's ever tried to move me when I get like that. My niece, Sarah, she came over once when I was just sitting in the kitchen. She brought me a lovely pot of African violets. You have to be so careful watering those, you know. If you get water on the leaves, they get all spotty and brown. I keep them on the windowsill above the sink so they get just enough light but not too much direct sun. The afternoon sun in that window can be terribly strong. It faded the curtains I made... I sewed them myself on my old Singer machine. The pedal on that machine sticks sometimes if you press too hard, and then it just runs away with you. I ruined a whole hem on a skirt once because of that pedal. It was a lovely navy blue wool skirt, too. Perfect for church in the winter. But anyway, Sarah just sat and waited for me to come back around. She didn't try to move my arm or anything, so I just don't know if I'm stiff or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Sad? My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he put out a bird feeder last spring... it was one of those wooden ones shaped like a little barn. He painted it red and everything. But the squirrels got into it right away. They're so clever, those squirrels, they just hang upside down by their little toes and eat all the sunflower seeds. He tried putting grease on the metal pole so they'd slide off, but it didn't even stop 'em. I told him he ought to just feed the squirrels, they gotta eat too, you know? They have those big bushy tails... I used to have a coat with a fur collar, kind of looked like a squirrel tail, but it was rabbit, I think. I wore it to church every winter until the lining ripped out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been speaking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking less? Well, I don't rightly know. I suppose I still talk to the ladies at church. And I talk to my cat, Barnaby. He's a big orange tabby, you know. Found him under the porch about five years ago... or maybe it was six. It was raining so hard that day, the street was practically a river. My basement flooded just a little bit, right by the washing machine. I had to put down all those old towels to soak it up. They were the green ones I bought at that discount store that closed down... the one next to the bakery. Oh, they had the best apple fritters there. I used to get one every Tuesday. But anyway, Barnaby just sat there on the porch step looking so pitiful, I had to bring him in. He really likes those little cans of tuna, but you can't give them too much or they get an upset stomach. So, I don't know if I speak less... I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. Well, except maybe the radio. I keep a little radio on the kitchen counter, just for some company, you know? It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, if someone tries to move your arm or hand, does it feel stiff or hard to move?\n\nPatient: Stiff? Well, I couldn't rightly say. Nobody's ever tried to move me when I get like that. My niece, Sarah, she came over once when I was just sitting in the kitchen. She brought me a lovely pot of African violets. You have to be so careful watering those, you know. If you get water on the leaves, they get all spotty and brown. I keep them on the windowsill above the sink so they get just enough light but not too much direct sun. The afternoon sun in that window can be terribly strong. It faded the curtains I made... I sewed them myself on my old Singer machine. The pedal on that machine sticks sometimes if you press too hard, and then it just runs away with you. I ruined a whole hem on a skirt once because of that pedal. It was a lovely navy blue wool skirt, too. Perfect for church in the winter. But anyway, Sarah just sat and waited for me to come back around. She didn't try to move my arm or anything, so I just don't know if I'm stiff or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Sad? Oh, no, I don't think so. I try to keep a smile on, you know? There's always something to be cheerful about if you look for it. Like my bird feeder out back. I put it up just so I could watch the cardinals in the morning while I have my tea. They are such a beautiful bright red. But those squirrels, I tell you, they are just absolute menaces! They climb right up the pole and steal all the expensive black oil sunflower seeds. \n\nMy neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he lives in the blue house next door, the one with the peeling paint on the porch railings—he told me to rub Crisco all over the metal pole so the squirrels would just slide right down. Well, I tried it, but it just made a terrible greasy mess of my hands and the patio. I usually only use Crisco when I'm baking pies. You have to make sure the shortening is real cold, and you only use ice water, otherwise the crust won't be flaky at all. My grandmother taught me that when I was just a little girl. She used to make the most wonderful strawberry rhubarb pies from the patch behind her barn. The rhubarb stalks were as thick as your wrist! We'd eat it warm on the back porch with a big scoop of vanilla ice cream melting all over the top... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Depressed? No, no, I don't get down in the dumps for days like that. I just keep busy with my little things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you keep the same posture for a while without moving at all?\n\nPatient: Posture? Oh, I couldn't really say for sure. I suppose I just stay however I was already sitting or standing. I don't think I'm holding a pose on purpose or anything. \n\nSpeaking of posture, my mother used to always tap me between the shoulder blades and say, \"Shoulders back, young lady!\" She was a real stickler for sitting up straight. And my teacher in the fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was exactly the same. We had those heavy old wooden desks at school, the kind where your seat was bolted right to the desk behind you. They even had the little round holes cut out for glass inkwells, though we only ever really used pencils. My father bought me a whole pack of yellow pencils from the five-and-dime once, and he'd sharpen every single one of them with his pocket knife. He always kept that little knife so sharp... he'd sit on the front porch in the evenings and whittle little boats out of pine scraps for me and my brother. We'd take them right down to the creek behind the house and watch the current take them away. That water was always so cold, even in the middle of July. We used to catch tadpoles in old glass mayonnaise jars... \n\nBut, oh, what were we talking about? Freezing? No, I don't know that I hold any special posture, I just sort of pause for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your face or voice has become less expressive or more flat than it used to be?\n\nPatient: My face? Oh, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I've always had a quiet sort of voice, I suppose. My husband, God rest his soul, he used to say I was as quiet as a church mouse when we first met. We met at a church social, actually. They had the most wonderful potluck dinners back then. Everyone would bring something in their best Pyrex dish. I always brought my famous green bean casserole with those crispy little fried onions on top. You have to make sure you bake it just long enough so the onions get golden brown, but not burnt. I burnt them once, right before Thanksgiving, and the whole house smelled like smoke for three days. We had to open all the windows, and it was freezing out! The wind was just howling through the trees in the front yard. We had this big old oak tree that would drop acorns all over the driveway, and it sounded like hail hitting the roof of the car...\n\nBut, no, I don't think my face or voice has changed. I'm just the same as I've always been.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt much more energetic or excited than usual, along with unusual thoughts or behavior?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Oh, goodness, no. I don't have that kind of energy anymore. I just take my time with things nowadays. I do like a good cup of coffee in the morning to get me going, though. I still use my old stovetop percolator. It makes that wonderful bubbling sound on the burner. I bought it at Miller's Hardware downtown... oh, it must have been forty years ago. Mr. Miller was such a sweet man. He always wore these heavy denim overalls and he kept a big wooden barrel of peppermint sticks right by the cash register. My brother used to suck on those peppermints until they were sharp as a needle, and then he'd poke me in the arm with it on the walk home. We had to walk two miles to town, right along the old dirt road, and the dust would just coat your shoes by the time you got there. My mother would make us take them off on the porch and beat them against the steps so we wouldn't track dirt into the kitchen. She was so particular about her floors. She used to scrub the linoleum on her hands and knees with a bucket of hot soapy water and a bristle brush... \n\nBut, oh, I am sorry, what were you asking? Excited? No, no, I don't get all worked up or energetic for days on end. Just slow and steady for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Someone else? Oh, no, I don't feel anything like that. It's just me in here. I don't think anyone is making me hold still. 

Speaking of holding still, my brother used to try and make me play statues with him when we were little. You know that game? You spin around and around until you're dizzy, and then you have to freeze right where you stop. He would always try to make me laugh so I'd move and lose the game. He had this funny little face he'd make, scrunching up his nose like a rabbit. We used to see wild rabbits in the garden all the time. My father planted this big patch of carrots and lettuce, and those rabbits would just help themselves. We had to put up a chicken wire fence all around it, but they'd still find a way to dig under. My father would get so frustrated, he'd sit out on the porch with his BB gun just waiting for them, but he never actually shot one. He was too soft-hearted for that, really. He brought home a stray dog once, a scruffy little terrier mix that just followed him home from the lumber yard. We named him Buster. Buster loved to chase those rabbits, but his legs were so short he never even got close. He'd come back panting, with his tongue hanging out, and just collapse on the braided rug in the kitchen...

But no, nobody is controlling me or making me freeze. I'm just... pausing, I suppose.
