What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I came in because the ladies at church said I should. I keep doing odd things lately, I guess. Like at the Sunday potluck, I was just standing by the long table holding the big serving spoon straight up in the air and not moving. Just frozen there. That spoon is real heavy, you know, it belonged to Pastor Miller’s mother before she passed. She used to grow the biggest tomatoes you ever saw in her backyard, right up against the fence. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the little green bugs got to 'em before they even turned red. \n\nBut anyway, they said people were waiting on me to serve the green beans, and I was just standing there holding it. Or another time, I was folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks were waiting on me to hand them out. They were those nice yellow paper napkins, kind of like the ones we had at my sister's 50th anniversary party... she wore a beautiful blue dress that day, or maybe it was purple... I can't quite recall. The cake was lemon, though. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, right. So I'm here because I just keep doing those strange things at the church.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or irritable for several days?\n\nPatient: Energized? Oh, no, not me. I don't have much energy at all anymore. I usually just sit and drink my coffee and watch the birds. We get these little blue jays in the yard. They sure are loud, though. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he put out a bird feeder last spring... it was one of those wooden ones shaped like a little barn. He painted it red and everything. But the squirrels got into it right away. They're so clever, those squirrels, they just hang upside down by their little toes and eat all the sunflower seeds. He tried putting grease on the metal pole so they'd slide off, but it didn't even stop 'em. I told him he ought to just feed the squirrels, they gotta eat too, you know? They have those big bushy tails... I used to have a coat with a fur collar, kind of looked like a squirrel tail, but it was rabbit, I think. I wore it to church every winter until the lining ripped out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been speaking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking less? Well, I don't rightly know. I suppose I still talk to the ladies at church. And I talk to my cat, Barnaby. He's a big orange tabby, you know. Found him under the porch about five years ago... or maybe it was six. It was raining so hard that day, the street was practically a river. My basement flooded just a little bit, right by the washing machine. I had to put down all those old towels to soak it up. They were the green ones I bought at that discount store that closed down... the one next to the bakery. Oh, they had the best apple fritters there. I used to get one every Tuesday. But anyway, Barnaby just sat there on the porch step looking so pitiful, I had to bring him in. He really likes those little cans of tuna, but you can't give them too much or they get an upset stomach. So, I don't know if I speak less... I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. Well, except maybe the radio. I keep a little radio on the kitchen counter, just for some company, you know? It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, if someone tries to move your arm or hand, does it feel stiff or hard to move?\n\nPatient: Stiff? Well, I couldn't rightly say. Nobody's ever tried to move me when I get like that. My niece, Sarah, she came over once when I was just sitting in the kitchen. She brought me a lovely pot of African violets. You have to be so careful watering those, you know. If you get water on the leaves, they get all spotty and brown. I keep them on the windowsill above the sink so they get just enough light but not too much direct sun. The afternoon sun in that window can be terribly strong. It faded the curtains I made... I sewed them myself on my old Singer machine. The pedal on that machine sticks sometimes if you press too hard, and then it just runs away with you. I ruined a whole hem on a skirt once because of that pedal. It was a lovely navy blue wool skirt, too. Perfect for church in the winter. But anyway, Sarah just sat and waited for me to come back around. She didn't try to move my arm or anything, so I just don't know if I'm stiff or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Sad? Oh, no, I don't think so. I try to keep a smile on, you know? There's always something to be cheerful about if you look for it. Like my bird feeder out back. I put it up just so I could watch the cardinals in the morning while I have my tea. They are such a beautiful bright red. But those squirrels, I tell you, they are just absolute menaces! They climb right up the pole and steal all the expensive black oil sunflower seeds. \n\nMy neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he lives in the blue house next door, the one with the peeling paint on the porch railings—he told me to rub Crisco all over the metal pole so the squirrels would just slide right down. Well, I tried it, but it just made a terrible greasy mess of my hands and the patio. I usually only use Crisco when I'm baking pies. You have to make sure the shortening is real cold, and you only use ice water, otherwise the crust won't be flaky at all. My grandmother taught me that when I was just a little girl. She used to make the most wonderful strawberry rhubarb pies from the patch behind her barn. The rhubarb stalks were as thick as your wrist! We'd eat it warm on the back porch with a big scoop of vanilla ice cream melting all over the top... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Depressed? No, no, I don't get down in the dumps for days like that. I just keep busy with my little things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you keep the same posture for a while without moving at all?\n\nPatient: Posture? Oh, I couldn't really say for sure. I suppose I just stay however I was already sitting or standing. I don't think I'm holding a pose on purpose or anything. \n\nSpeaking of posture, my mother used to always tap me between the shoulder blades and say, \"Shoulders back, young lady!\" She was a real stickler for sitting up straight. And my teacher in the fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was exactly the same. We had those heavy old wooden desks at school, the kind where your seat was bolted right to the desk behind you. They even had the little round holes cut out for glass inkwells, though we only ever really used pencils. My father bought me a whole pack of yellow pencils from the five-and-dime once, and he'd sharpen every single one of them with his pocket knife. He always kept that little knife so sharp... he'd sit on the front porch in the evenings and whittle little boats out of pine scraps for me and my brother. We'd take them right down to the creek behind the house and watch the current take them away. That water was always so cold, even in the middle of July. We used to catch tadpoles in old glass mayonnaise jars... \n\nBut, oh, what were we talking about? Freezing? No, I don't know that I hold any special posture, I just sort of pause for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your face or voice has become less expressive or more flat than it used to be?\n\nPatient: My face? Oh, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I've always had a quiet sort of voice, I suppose. My husband, God rest his soul, he used to say I was as quiet as a church mouse when we first met. We met at a church social, actually. They had the most wonderful potluck dinners back then. Everyone would bring something in their best Pyrex dish. I always brought my famous green bean casserole with those crispy little fried onions on top. You have to make sure you bake it just long enough so the onions get golden brown, but not burnt. I burnt them once, right before Thanksgiving, and the whole house smelled like smoke for three days. We had to open all the windows, and it was freezing out! The wind was just howling through the trees in the front yard. We had this big old oak tree that would drop acorns all over the driveway, and it sounded like hail hitting the roof of the car...\n\nBut, no, I don't think my face or voice has changed. I'm just the same as I've always been.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt much more energetic or excited than usual, along with unusual thoughts or behavior?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Oh, goodness, no. I don't have that kind of energy anymore. I just take my time with things nowadays. I do like a good cup of coffee in the morning to get me going, though. I still use my old stovetop percolator. It makes that wonderful bubbling sound on the burner. I bought it at Miller's Hardware downtown... oh, it must have been forty years ago. Mr. Miller was such a sweet man. He always wore these heavy denim overalls and he kept a big wooden barrel of peppermint sticks right by the cash register. My brother used to suck on those peppermints until they were sharp as a needle, and then he'd poke me in the arm with it on the walk home. We had to walk two miles to town, right along the old dirt road, and the dust would just coat your shoes by the time you got there. My mother would make us take them off on the porch and beat them against the steps so we wouldn't track dirt into the kitchen. She was so particular about her floors. She used to scrub the linoleum on her hands and knees with a bucket of hot soapy water and a bristle brush... \n\nBut, oh, I am sorry, what were you asking? Excited? No, no, I don't get all worked up or energetic for days on end. Just slow and steady for me.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you feel as if someone else is controlling your body or making you hold still?\n\nPatient: Someone else? Oh, no, I don't feel anything like that. It's just me in here. I don't think anyone is making me hold still. \n\nSpeaking of holding still, my brother used to try and make me play statues with him when we were little. You know that game? You spin around and around until you're dizzy, and then you have to freeze right where you stop. He would always try to make me laugh so I'd move and lose the game. He had this funny little face he'd make, scrunching up his nose like a rabbit. We used to see wild rabbits in the garden all the time. My father planted this big patch of carrots and lettuce, and those rabbits would just help themselves. We had to put up a chicken wire fence all around it, but they'd still find a way to dig under. My father would get so frustrated, he'd sit out on the porch with his BB gun just waiting for them, but he never actually shot one. He was too soft-hearted for that, really. He brought home a stray dog once, a scruffy little terrier mix that just followed him home from the lumber yard. We named him Buster. Buster loved to chase those rabbits, but his legs were so short he never even got close. He'd come back panting, with his tongue hanging out, and just collapse on the braided rug in the kitchen...\n\nBut no, nobody is controlling me or making me freeze. I'm just... pausing, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you ever do it on purpose or feel like you’re making yourself hold still?\n\nPatient: On purpose? Oh, no, I don't make myself do it. I'm not trying to hold still on purpose. It's not like when you have to sit for a photograph and try not to blink. My uncle Arthur had one of those big old cameras with the flashbulbs that popped so bright you'd see spots for five minutes afterward. He made us all sit on the front steps for a family portrait one Easter Sunday. I had this lovely little yellow dress my mother made for me. She was a wonderful seamstress, you know. She didn't even use a pattern half the time, she'd just measure you with a piece of string and start cutting the cloth. She used to save all the little fabric scraps in a big round tin that used to have shortbread cookies in it. The tin had a picture of a Scottish terrier on the lid, wearing a little red plaid collar. We kept all sorts of things in tins back then. Buttons, safety pins, loose change... my father kept his pipe tobacco in an old red Folgers coffee can out in the shed. You could smell that sweet cherry tobacco before you even opened the door... \n\nBut, no, I'm not making myself freeze. It just happens, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, can someone else get you moving again by telling you to move, or does that not help?\n\nPatient: Get me moving? I'm not really sure... I don't think I've ever noticed if someone telling me to move makes a difference. My hearing isn't what it used to be, anyway. Sometimes people have to repeat themselves two or three times before I catch what they're saying. My husband, bless his heart, he used to mumble terribly when he read the evening paper. He'd sit in his brown recliner—the one with the little tear on the right armrest where the cat scratched it—and he'd just mutter about the headlines. That cat, oh, she was a terror. A calico named Patches. We found her in a cardboard box behind the grocery store in the pouring rain. She was so tiny she fit right in the palm of my hand. I fed her warm milk with a little eye dropper for the first two weeks. She grew up to be the most demanding creature, always meowing for her supper right at five o'clock. If you were even a minute late, she'd weave between your legs until you nearly tripped over her. We had this yellow linoleum in the kitchen back then, and I was always terrified I'd slip and fall while holding her food dish... \n\nBut I don't know if someone telling me to move helps. I just haven't paid attention to that.\n\nDoctor: When you get frozen like that, do you ever feel compelled to hold that posture because someone or something is telling you to?\n\nPatient: Told to hold still? Oh, no, I don't hear anything telling me to do that. It's not an instruction or a voice or anything like that. It's funny you mention being told what to do, though. My grandmother used to give the most specific instructions when she was baking. She wouldn't let anyone else in the kitchen when she was making her apple pies. She said we'd ruin the temperature of the room and the crust wouldn't be flaky. She used this enormous wooden rolling pin that had belonged to her mother, and she'd roll the dough out on a piece of wax paper dusted with flour. She always used Granny Smith apples, said the tartness was the secret. She'd peel them so carefully, trying to get the whole peel off in one long green ribbon. My sister and I used to take the apple peels and throw them over our shoulders onto the floor. There was some old wives' tale that whatever letter the peel formed when it landed would be the first initial of the man you were going to marry. Mine always just looked like a squiggly line, or maybe an 'S' if you squinted. I ended up marrying a William, so I suppose the apple peel didn't know what it was talking about anyway... \n\nBut no, nobody is telling me to hold that posture. I just kind of get stuck, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have beliefs that other people thought were untrue or unusual?\n\nPatient: Untrue beliefs? No, I don't think anyone has ever told me that. I just believe what's normal, you know? Not like my old neighbor, Mrs. Gable—now she had some unusual beliefs. She swore up and down you had to plant your garden by the signs of the moon. She kept this worn-out Farmer's Almanac right on her kitchen counter, tucked behind a ceramic rooster that held all her wooden spoons. If the moon wasn't in the right phase, she wouldn't put a single seed in the dirt, even if it was a perfectly warm day in May and the ground was ready. She did grow the biggest beefsteak tomatoes, though, I'll give her that. We'd slice them thick and put them on soft white bread with just a little mayonnaise and a dash of salt. Oh, it was the best sandwich you could ever eat in the summertime. We used to sit out on her porch swing, eating our tomato sandwiches and watching the cars go by. There weren't nearly as many cars on the road back then. Mostly it was just Mr. Henderson driving his old blue pickup truck down to the feed store. He always had his golden retriever riding in the back, tongue just flapping in the wind... \n\nBut, no, nobody says my beliefs are untrue. I just mind my own business, really.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, if someone tries to move you, do you resist them?\n\nPatient: Resist them? I... I don't know. I don't think anyone has really tried to move me when I'm like that. Mostly people just leave me be. My sister used to be the one who wouldn't leave well enough alone, always trying to fix things that weren't broken. Like that time she tried to cut her own bangs right before our cousin's wedding. She used these big, heavy fabric shears that my mother kept hidden in a tin box under the sewing machine. Mother was furious, of course. She said those scissors were only for cutting dress patterns and now they'd be dull. My sister ended up with her hair chopped straight across, about an inch above her eyebrows. She looked like a startled owl in all the wedding photographs. It was a beautiful wedding, though. They had it in a little white church with stained glass windows that caught the afternoon sun just perfectly. They served a white cake with buttercream frosting that melted right on your tongue... \n\nBut no, I don't think anyone tries to push me or move my arms or anything. So I couldn't say if I resist.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Unusually sad? Oh, I don't think so. I mean, everybody gets a little down sometimes, but not for days and days. Not like my Uncle Arthur. Now, he was a sad sort of man. He used to sit in this big, green leather recliner in the corner of his living room, just staring out the window for hours. He had this little transistor radio he kept on the side table, always tuned to the baseball game, but I don't think he was really listening. He smoked a pipe, too—cherry tobacco. The whole house smelled like it. Sweet, but kind of heavy, you know? He had this old hound dog named Buster that would sleep right at his feet. Buster was mostly blind by the time I was old enough to remember him, and he had this funny way of walking where his back legs kind of went sideways. We used to try to get Buster to chase after a tennis ball, but he'd just look at us and lay his head back down on his paws... \n\nBut no, I don't get sad like that for days on end. I just keep busy, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices talking to you or about you when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, I don't hear any voices. It's usually pretty quiet around here. Too quiet sometimes, if you ask me. I used to love listening to the radio when I was a girl. We had this big wooden console radio right in the parlor, and on Sunday nights my father would tune it in to hear the mystery programs. The Shadow, that was his favorite. He'd sit in his rocking chair, the one with the woven cane seat that squeaked every time he leaned back. Squeak, squeak, squeak. My mother hated that noise. She tried oiling the runners once with some sewing machine oil, but it just made a mess on the braided rug. She made that rug herself, you know, from old wool coats and torn skirts. It took her the better part of a winter to finish it, sitting by the wood stove with her big wooden needle. She had hands that were always busy, mother did. Even when she was just sitting, she was knitting or darning socks... \n\nBut no, no voices talking to me. Just my own thoughts, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arm or hand while you’re frozen, does it stay in that position without much resistance?\n\nPatient: Stay in that position? Well... I'm not really sure about that. Like I said before, nobody really bothers me when I get like that. They just let me be. Which is nice, I suppose. People used to be a lot more hands-on, didn't they? I remember when I was a little girl, the school nurse, Mrs. Gable, she was always poking and prodding at us. She had these freezing cold hands. She'd check our hair for lice with these two wooden sticks, just parting the hair over and over. She smelled like peppermint and rubbing alcohol. She lived in this little blue house right at the end of Elm Street, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree must have been a hundred years old. In the autumn, the leaves would turn this brilliant, fiery red, and they'd cover the whole lawn. We used to rake them up into these massive piles and just jump right in. My brother lost his shoe in one of those piles once. It was a brand new Buster Brown shoe, too. Mother made him stay out there until it got dark looking for it, but he never found it... \n\nBut anyway, I couldn't tell you if my arm stays put. No one's ever tried it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I ended up marrying a William, so I suppose the apple peel didn't know what it was talking about anyway... \n\nBut no, nobody is telling me to hold that posture. I just kind of get stuck, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have beliefs that other people thought were untrue or unusual?\n\nPatient: Untrue beliefs? No, I don't think anyone has ever told me that. I just believe what's normal, you know? Not like my old neighbor, Mrs. Gable—now she had some unusual beliefs. She swore up and down you had to plant your garden by the signs of the moon. She kept this worn-out Farmer's Almanac right on her kitchen counter, tucked behind a ceramic rooster that held all her wooden spoons. If the moon wasn't in the right phase, she wouldn't put a single seed in the dirt, even if it was a perfectly warm day in May and the ground was ready. She did grow the biggest beefsteak tomatoes, though, I'll give her that. We'd slice them thick and put them on soft white bread with just a little mayonnaise and a dash of salt. Oh, it was the best sandwich you could ever eat in the summertime. We used to sit out on her porch swing, eating our tomato sandwiches and watching the cars go by. There weren't nearly as many cars on the road back then. Mostly it was just Mr. Henderson driving his old blue pickup truck down to the feed store. He always had his golden retriever riding in the back, tongue just flapping in the wind... \n\nBut, no, nobody says my beliefs are untrue. I just mind my own business, really.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, if someone tries to move you, do you resist them?\n\nPatient: Resist them? I... I don't know. I don't think anyone has really tried to move me when I'm like that. Mostly people just leave me be. My sister used to be the one who wouldn't leave well enough alone, always trying to fix things that weren't broken. Like that time she tried to cut her own bangs right before our cousin's wedding. She used these big, heavy fabric shears that my mother kept hidden in a tin box under the sewing machine. Mother was furious, of course. She said those scissors were only for cutting dress patterns and now they'd be dull. My sister ended up with her hair chopped straight across, about an inch above her eyebrows. She looked like a startled owl in all the wedding photographs. It was a beautiful wedding, though. They had it in a little white church with stained glass windows that caught the afternoon sun just perfectly. They served a white cake with buttercream frosting that melted right on your tongue... \n\nBut no, I don't think anyone tries to push me or move my arms or anything. So I couldn't say if I resist.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Unusually sad? Oh, I don't think so. I mean, everybody gets a little down sometimes, but not for days and days. Not like my Uncle Arthur. Now, he was a sad sort of man. He used to sit in this big, green leather recliner in the corner of his living room, just staring out the window for hours. He had this little transistor radio he kept on the side table, always tuned to the baseball game, but I don't think he was really listening. He smoked a pipe, too—cherry tobacco. The whole house smelled like it. Sweet, but kind of heavy, you know? He had this old hound dog named Buster that would sleep right at his feet. Buster was mostly blind by the time I was old enough to remember him, and he had this funny way of walking where his back legs kind of went sideways. We used to try to get Buster to chase after a tennis ball, but he'd just look at us and lay his head back down on his paws... \n\nBut no, I don't get sad like that for days on end. I just keep busy, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices talking to you or about you when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, I don't hear any voices. It's usually pretty quiet around here. Too quiet sometimes, if you ask me. I used to love listening to the radio when I was a girl. We had this big wooden console radio right in the parlor, and on Sunday nights my father would tune it in to hear the mystery programs. The Shadow, that was his favorite. He'd sit in his rocking chair, the one with the woven cane seat that squeaked every time he leaned back. Squeak, squeak, squeak. My mother hated that noise. She tried oiling the runners once with some sewing machine oil, but it just made a mess on the braided rug. She made that rug herself, you know, from old wool coats and torn skirts. It took her the better part of a winter to finish it, sitting by the wood stove with her big wooden needle. She had hands that were always busy, mother did. Even when she was just sitting, she was knitting or darning socks... \n\nBut no, no voices talking to me. Just my own thoughts, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arm or hand while you’re frozen, does it stay in that position without much resistance?\n\nPatient: Stay in that position? Well... I'm not really sure about that. Like I said before, nobody really bothers me when I get like that. They just let me be. Which is nice, I suppose. People used to be a lot more hands-on, didn't they? I remember when I was a little girl, the school nurse, Mrs. Gable, she was always poking and prodding at us. She had these freezing cold hands. She'd check our hair for lice with these two wooden sticks, just parting the hair over and over. She smelled like peppermint and rubbing alcohol. She lived in this little blue house right at the end of Elm Street, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree must have been a hundred years old. In the autumn, the leaves would turn this brilliant, fiery red, and they'd cover the whole lawn. We used to rake them up into these massive piles and just jump right in. My brother lost his shoe in one of those piles once. It was a brand new Buster Brown shoe, too. Mother made him stay out there until it got dark looking for it, but he never found it... \n\nBut anyway, I couldn't tell you if my arm stays put. No one's ever tried it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Like wax? Well, I don't know about that. I just told you, nobody really comes around to move my arms when I'm stuck like that. Wax, though... that reminds me of my Aunt Clara. She used to make her own candles, you know. Beeswax. She had these big blocks of it she kept in the pantry, and she'd melt it down on the stove in this old, dented double boiler. The whole kitchen would smell like honey and smoke. She'd pour it into these little tin molds, and we had to be so careful not to bump the table while they were setting. She made a batch once for Christmas, dyed them bright red with some kind of powder she got from the apothecary. They burned so pretty, but the wax dripped all over the good lace tablecloth. Mother was furious. She tried freezing the cloth and scraping the wax off with a butter knife, but it left this pink stain right in the middle. We had to put a big bowl of pinecones over it every time we had company... 

But no, nobody moves my arms, so I couldn't say if they stay like wax.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF06.1, F20.2, F20.9, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I came in because the ladies at church said I should. I keep doing odd things lately, I guess. Like at the Sunday potluck, I was just standing by the long table holding the big serving spoon straight up in the air and not moving. Just frozen there. That spoon is real heavy, you know, it belonged to Pastor Miller’s mother before she passed. She used to grow the biggest tomatoes you ever saw in her backyard, right up against the fence. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the little green bugs got to 'em before they even turned red. \n\nBut anyway, they said people were waiting on me to serve the green beans, and I was just standing there holding it. Or another time, I was folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks were waiting on me to hand them out. They were those nice yellow paper napkins, kind of like the ones we had at my sister's 50th anniversary party... she wore a beautiful blue dress that day, or maybe it was purple... I can't quite recall. The cake was lemon, though. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, right. So I'm here because I just keep doing those strange things at the church.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or irritable for several days?\n\nPatient: Energized? Oh, no, not me. I don't have much energy at all anymore. I usually just sit and drink my coffee and watch the birds. We get these little blue jays in the yard. They sure are loud, though. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he put out a bird feeder last spring... it was one of those wooden ones shaped like a little barn. He painted it red and everything. But the squirrels got into it right away. They're so clever, those squirrels, they just hang upside down by their little toes and eat all the sunflower seeds. He tried putting grease on the metal pole so they'd slide off, but it didn't even stop 'em. I told him he ought to just feed the squirrels, they gotta eat too, you know? They have those big bushy tails... I used to have a coat with a fur collar, kind of looked like a squirrel tail, but it was rabbit, I think. I wore it to church every winter until the lining ripped out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been speaking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking less? Well, I don't rightly know. I suppose I still talk to the ladies at church. And I talk to my cat, Barnaby. He's a big orange tabby, you know. Found him under the porch about five years ago... or maybe it was six. It was raining so hard that day, the street was practically a river. My basement flooded just a little bit, right by the washing machine. I had to put down all those old towels to soak it up. They were the green ones I bought at that discount store that closed down... the one next to the bakery. Oh, they had the best apple fritters there. I used to get one every Tuesday. But anyway, Barnaby just sat there on the porch step looking so pitiful, I had to bring him in. He really likes those little cans of tuna, but you can't give them too much or they get an upset stomach. So, I don't know if I speak less... I just talk to whoever is around, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, are you aware of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Aware? Well, I don't rightly know. I'm not sure I notice much of anything right then. It's just sort of... still. Like how it gets real quiet after a big snowstorm, you know? We had a terrible blizzard back in, oh, must have been '78 or so. The snow was piled up clear past the front windows! We couldn't even get the front door open. My husband, God rest his soul, he had to climb right out the living room window just to shovel us out. He had this big metal shovel with a wooden handle, but the handle splintered right down the middle because that snow was so heavy and wet. He tried fixing it up with some of that silver tape, but it was never really the same after that. You really need a good sturdy shovel for that kind of wet snow, otherwise it just breaks your back. But, um... what was it? Oh, no, I just don't really know if I hear the ladies talking when I get stuck like that. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, goodness no. I don't hear anybody that isn't there. Well, except maybe the radio. I keep a little radio on the kitchen counter, just for some company, you know? It's one of those older ones with the dial you have to turn real careful to get the station right. If you bump it even a little bit, it gets all static-y. I bought it at a rummage sale over at the Methodist church... oh, must be ten years ago now. They had tables and tables of things set up in the gymnasium. I almost bought a beautiful porcelain gravy boat that day, but it had a little chip right on the spout. It's a shame when nice things get chipped like that. My mother had a whole set of china with little blue cornflowers on it, and she'd only bring it out for Thanksgiving. We always had a big turkey, and my uncle Arthur would carve it. He had this special electric knife that made an awful buzzing sound... scared the dog half to death every time he turned it on. We had a little beagle back then named Buster. He loved turkey scraps. But no, I don't hear any voices or see anything unusual, just my regular things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, if someone tries to move your arm or hand, does it feel stiff or hard to move?\n\nPatient: Stiff? Well, I couldn't rightly say. Nobody's ever tried to move me when I get like that. My niece, Sarah, she came over once when I was just sitting in the kitchen. She brought me a lovely pot of African violets. You have to be so careful watering those, you know. If you get water on the leaves, they get all spotty and brown. I keep them on the windowsill above the sink so they get just enough light but not too much direct sun. The afternoon sun in that window can be terribly strong. It faded the curtains I made... I sewed them myself on my old Singer machine. The pedal on that machine sticks sometimes if you press too hard, and then it just runs away with you. I ruined a whole hem on a skirt once because of that pedal. It was a lovely navy blue wool skirt, too. Perfect for church in the winter. But anyway, Sarah just sat and waited for me to come back around. She didn't try to move my arm or anything, so I just don't know if I'm stiff or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Sad? Oh, no, I don't think so. I try to keep a smile on, you know? There's always something to be cheerful about if you look for it. Like my bird feeder out back. I put it up just so I could watch the cardinals in the morning while I have my tea. They are such a beautiful bright red. But those squirrels, I tell you, they are just absolute menaces! They climb right up the pole and steal all the expensive black oil sunflower seeds. \n\nMy neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he lives in the blue house next door, the one with the peeling paint on the porch railings—he told me to rub Crisco all over the metal pole so the squirrels would just slide right down. Well, I tried it, but it just made a terrible greasy mess of my hands and the patio. I usually only use Crisco when I'm baking pies. You have to make sure the shortening is real cold, and you only use ice water, otherwise the crust won't be flaky at all. My grandmother taught me that when I was just a little girl. She used to make the most wonderful strawberry rhubarb pies from the patch behind her barn. The rhubarb stalks were as thick as your wrist! We'd eat it warm on the back porch with a big scoop of vanilla ice cream melting all over the top... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Depressed? No, no, I don't get down in the dumps for days like that. I just keep busy with my little things.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you keep the same posture for a while without moving at all?\n\nPatient: Posture? Oh, I couldn't really say for sure. I suppose I just stay however I was already sitting or standing. I don't think I'm holding a pose on purpose or anything. \n\nSpeaking of posture, my mother used to always tap me between the shoulder blades and say, \"Shoulders back, young lady!\" She was a real stickler for sitting up straight. And my teacher in the fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was exactly the same. We had those heavy old wooden desks at school, the kind where your seat was bolted right to the desk behind you. They even had the little round holes cut out for glass inkwells, though we only ever really used pencils. My father bought me a whole pack of yellow pencils from the five-and-dime once, and he'd sharpen every single one of them with his pocket knife. He always kept that little knife so sharp... he'd sit on the front porch in the evenings and whittle little boats out of pine scraps for me and my brother. We'd take them right down to the creek behind the house and watch the current take them away. That water was always so cold, even in the middle of July. We used to catch tadpoles in old glass mayonnaise jars... \n\nBut, oh, what were we talking about? Freezing? No, I don't know that I hold any special posture, I just sort of pause for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your face or voice has become less expressive or more flat than it used to be?\n\nPatient: My face? Oh, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I've always had a quiet sort of voice, I suppose. My husband, God rest his soul, he used to say I was as quiet as a church mouse when we first met. We met at a church social, actually. They had the most wonderful potluck dinners back then. Everyone would bring something in their best Pyrex dish. I always brought my famous green bean casserole with those crispy little fried onions on top. You have to make sure you bake it just long enough so the onions get golden brown, but not burnt. I burnt them once, right before Thanksgiving, and the whole house smelled like smoke for three days. We had to open all the windows, and it was freezing out! The wind was just howling through the trees in the front yard. We had this big old oak tree that would drop acorns all over the driveway, and it sounded like hail hitting the roof of the car...\n\nBut, no, I don't think my face or voice has changed. I'm just the same as I've always been.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt much more energetic or excited than usual, along with unusual thoughts or behavior?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Oh, goodness, no. I don't have that kind of energy anymore. I just take my time with things nowadays. I do like a good cup of coffee in the morning to get me going, though. I still use my old stovetop percolator. It makes that wonderful bubbling sound on the burner. I bought it at Miller's Hardware downtown... oh, it must have been forty years ago. Mr. Miller was such a sweet man. He always wore these heavy denim overalls and he kept a big wooden barrel of peppermint sticks right by the cash register. My brother used to suck on those peppermints until they were sharp as a needle, and then he'd poke me in the arm with it on the walk home. We had to walk two miles to town, right along the old dirt road, and the dust would just coat your shoes by the time you got there. My mother would make us take them off on the porch and beat them against the steps so we wouldn't track dirt into the kitchen. She was so particular about her floors. She used to scrub the linoleum on her hands and knees with a bucket of hot soapy water and a bristle brush... \n\nBut, oh, I am sorry, what were you asking? Excited? No, no, I don't get all worked up or energetic for days on end. Just slow and steady for me.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you feel as if someone else is controlling your body or making you hold still?\n\nPatient: Someone else? Oh, no, I don't feel anything like that. It's just me in here. I don't think anyone is making me hold still. \n\nSpeaking of holding still, my brother used to try and make me play statues with him when we were little. You know that game? You spin around and around until you're dizzy, and then you have to freeze right where you stop. He would always try to make me laugh so I'd move and lose the game. He had this funny little face he'd make, scrunching up his nose like a rabbit. We used to see wild rabbits in the garden all the time. My father planted this big patch of carrots and lettuce, and those rabbits would just help themselves. We had to put up a chicken wire fence all around it, but they'd still find a way to dig under. My father would get so frustrated, he'd sit out on the porch with his BB gun just waiting for them, but he never actually shot one. He was too soft-hearted for that, really. He brought home a stray dog once, a scruffy little terrier mix that just followed him home from the lumber yard. We named him Buster. Buster loved to chase those rabbits, but his legs were so short he never even got close. He'd come back panting, with his tongue hanging out, and just collapse on the braided rug in the kitchen...\n\nBut no, nobody is controlling me or making me freeze. I'm just... pausing, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you ever do it on purpose or feel like you’re making yourself hold still?\n\nPatient: On purpose? Oh, no, I don't make myself do it. I'm not trying to hold still on purpose. It's not like when you have to sit for a photograph and try not to blink. My uncle Arthur had one of those big old cameras with the flashbulbs that popped so bright you'd see spots for five minutes afterward. He made us all sit on the front steps for a family portrait one Easter Sunday. I had this lovely little yellow dress my mother made for me. She was a wonderful seamstress, you know. She didn't even use a pattern half the time, she'd just measure you with a piece of string and start cutting the cloth. She used to save all the little fabric scraps in a big round tin that used to have shortbread cookies in it. The tin had a picture of a Scottish terrier on the lid, wearing a little red plaid collar. We kept all sorts of things in tins back then. Buttons, safety pins, loose change... my father kept his pipe tobacco in an old red Folgers coffee can out in the shed. You could smell that sweet cherry tobacco before you even opened the door... \n\nBut, no, I'm not making myself freeze. It just happens, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, can someone else get you moving again by telling you to move, or does that not help?\n\nPatient: Get me moving? I'm not really sure... I don't think I've ever noticed if someone telling me to move makes a difference. My hearing isn't what it used to be, anyway. Sometimes people have to repeat themselves two or three times before I catch what they're saying. My husband, bless his heart, he used to mumble terribly when he read the evening paper. He'd sit in his brown recliner—the one with the little tear on the right armrest where the cat scratched it—and he'd just mutter about the headlines. That cat, oh, she was a terror. A calico named Patches. We found her in a cardboard box behind the grocery store in the pouring rain. She was so tiny she fit right in the palm of my hand. I fed her warm milk with a little eye dropper for the first two weeks. She grew up to be the most demanding creature, always meowing for her supper right at five o'clock. If you were even a minute late, she'd weave between your legs until you nearly tripped over her. We had this yellow linoleum in the kitchen back then, and I was always terrified I'd slip and fall while holding her food dish... \n\nBut I don't know if someone telling me to move helps. I just haven't paid attention to that.\n\nDoctor: When you get frozen like that, do you ever feel compelled to hold that posture because someone or something is telling you to?\n\nPatient: Told to hold still? Oh, no, I don't hear anything telling me to do that. It's not an instruction or a voice or anything like that. It's funny you mention being told what to do, though. My grandmother used to give the most specific instructions when she was baking. She wouldn't let anyone else in the kitchen when she was making her apple pies. She said we'd ruin the temperature of the room and the crust wouldn't be flaky. She used this enormous wooden rolling pin that had belonged to her mother, and she'd roll the dough out on a piece of wax paper dusted with flour. She always used Granny Smith apples, said the tartness was the secret. She'd peel them so carefully, trying to get the whole peel off in one long green ribbon. My sister and I used to take the apple peels and throw them over our shoulders onto the floor. There was some old wives' tale that whatever letter the peel formed when it landed would be the first initial of the man you were going to marry. Mine always just looked like a squiggly line, or maybe an 'S' if you squinted. I ended up marrying a William, so I suppose the apple peel didn't know what it was talking about anyway... \n\nBut no, nobody is telling me to hold that posture. I just kind of get stuck, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have beliefs that other people thought were untrue or unusual?\n\nPatient: Untrue beliefs? No, I don't think anyone has ever told me that. I just believe what's normal, you know? Not like my old neighbor, Mrs. Gable—now she had some unusual beliefs. She swore up and down you had to plant your garden by the signs of the moon. She kept this worn-out Farmer's Almanac right on her kitchen counter, tucked behind a ceramic rooster that held all her wooden spoons. If the moon wasn't in the right phase, she wouldn't put a single seed in the dirt, even if it was a perfectly warm day in May and the ground was ready. She did grow the biggest beefsteak tomatoes, though, I'll give her that. We'd slice them thick and put them on soft white bread with just a little mayonnaise and a dash of salt. Oh, it was the best sandwich you could ever eat in the summertime. We used to sit out on her porch swing, eating our tomato sandwiches and watching the cars go by. There weren't nearly as many cars on the road back then. Mostly it was just Mr. Henderson driving his old blue pickup truck down to the feed store. He always had his golden retriever riding in the back, tongue just flapping in the wind... \n\nBut, no, nobody says my beliefs are untrue. I just mind my own business, really.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, if someone tries to move you, do you resist them?\n\nPatient: Resist them? I... I don't know. I don't think anyone has really tried to move me when I'm like that. Mostly people just leave me be. My sister used to be the one who wouldn't leave well enough alone, always trying to fix things that weren't broken. Like that time she tried to cut her own bangs right before our cousin's wedding. She used these big, heavy fabric shears that my mother kept hidden in a tin box under the sewing machine. Mother was furious, of course. She said those scissors were only for cutting dress patterns and now they'd be dull. My sister ended up with her hair chopped straight across, about an inch above her eyebrows. She looked like a startled owl in all the wedding photographs. It was a beautiful wedding, though. They had it in a little white church with stained glass windows that caught the afternoon sun just perfectly. They served a white cake with buttercream frosting that melted right on your tongue... \n\nBut no, I don't think anyone tries to push me or move my arms or anything. So I couldn't say if I resist.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Unusually sad? Oh, I don't think so. I mean, everybody gets a little down sometimes, but not for days and days. Not like my Uncle Arthur. Now, he was a sad sort of man. He used to sit in this big, green leather recliner in the corner of his living room, just staring out the window for hours. He had this little transistor radio he kept on the side table, always tuned to the baseball game, but I don't think he was really listening. He smoked a pipe, too—cherry tobacco. The whole house smelled like it. Sweet, but kind of heavy, you know? He had this old hound dog named Buster that would sleep right at his feet. Buster was mostly blind by the time I was old enough to remember him, and he had this funny way of walking where his back legs kind of went sideways. We used to try to get Buster to chase after a tennis ball, but he'd just look at us and lay his head back down on his paws... \n\nBut no, I don't get sad like that for days on end. I just keep busy, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices talking to you or about you when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, I don't hear any voices. It's usually pretty quiet around here. Too quiet sometimes, if you ask me. I used to love listening to the radio when I was a girl. We had this big wooden console radio right in the parlor, and on Sunday nights my father would tune it in to hear the mystery programs. The Shadow, that was his favorite. He'd sit in his rocking chair, the one with the woven cane seat that squeaked every time he leaned back. Squeak, squeak, squeak. My mother hated that noise. She tried oiling the runners once with some sewing machine oil, but it just made a mess on the braided rug. She made that rug herself, you know, from old wool coats and torn skirts. It took her the better part of a winter to finish it, sitting by the wood stove with her big wooden needle. She had hands that were always busy, mother did. Even when she was just sitting, she was knitting or darning socks... \n\nBut no, no voices talking to me. Just my own thoughts, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arm or hand while you’re frozen, does it stay in that position without much resistance?\n\nPatient: Stay in that position? Well... I'm not really sure about that. Like I said before, nobody really bothers me when I get like that. They just let me be. Which is nice, I suppose. People used to be a lot more hands-on, didn't they? I remember when I was a little girl, the school nurse, Mrs. Gable, she was always poking and prodding at us. She had these freezing cold hands. She'd check our hair for lice with these two wooden sticks, just parting the hair over and over. She smelled like peppermint and rubbing alcohol. She lived in this little blue house right at the end of Elm Street, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree must have been a hundred years old. In the autumn, the leaves would turn this brilliant, fiery red, and they'd cover the whole lawn. We used to rake them up into these massive piles and just jump right in. My brother lost his shoe in one of those piles once. It was a brand new Buster Brown shoe, too. Mother made him stay out there until it got dark looking for it, but he never found it... \n\nBut anyway, I couldn't tell you if my arm stays put. No one's ever tried it.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arm while you are frozen, does it stay in that position like wax for a while?\n\nPatient: Like wax? Well, I don't know about that. I just told you, nobody really comes around to move my arms when I'm stuck like that. Wax, though... that reminds me of my Aunt Clara. She used to make her own candles, you know. Beeswax. She had these big blocks of it she kept in the pantry, and she'd melt it down on the stove in this old, dented double boiler. The whole kitchen would smell like honey and smoke. She'd pour it into these little tin molds, and we had to be so careful not to bump the table while they were setting. She made a batch once for Christmas, dyed them bright red with some kind of powder she got from the apothecary. They burned so pretty, but the wax dripped all over the good lace tablecloth. Mother was furious. She tried freezing the cloth and scraping the wax off with a butter knife, but it left this pink stain right in the middle. We had to put a big bowl of pinecones over it every time we had company... \n\nBut no, nobody moves my arms, so I couldn't say if they stay like wax.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Buster loved to chase those rabbits, but his legs were so short he never even got close. He'd come back panting, with his tongue hanging out, and just collapse on the braided rug in the kitchen...\n\nBut no, nobody is controlling me or making me freeze. I'm just... pausing, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze like that, do you ever do it on purpose or feel like you’re making yourself hold still?\n\nPatient: On purpose? Oh, no, I don't make myself do it. I'm not trying to hold still on purpose. It's not like when you have to sit for a photograph and try not to blink. My uncle Arthur had one of those big old cameras with the flashbulbs that popped so bright you'd see spots for five minutes afterward. He made us all sit on the front steps for a family portrait one Easter Sunday. I had this lovely little yellow dress my mother made for me. She was a wonderful seamstress, you know. She didn't even use a pattern half the time, she'd just measure you with a piece of string and start cutting the cloth. She used to save all the little fabric scraps in a big round tin that used to have shortbread cookies in it. The tin had a picture of a Scottish terrier on the lid, wearing a little red plaid collar. We kept all sorts of things in tins back then. Buttons, safety pins, loose change... my father kept his pipe tobacco in an old red Folgers coffee can out in the shed. You could smell that sweet cherry tobacco before you even opened the door... \n\nBut, no, I'm not making myself freeze. It just happens, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, can someone else get you moving again by telling you to move, or does that not help?\n\nPatient: Get me moving? I'm not really sure... I don't think I've ever noticed if someone telling me to move makes a difference. My hearing isn't what it used to be, anyway. Sometimes people have to repeat themselves two or three times before I catch what they're saying. My husband, bless his heart, he used to mumble terribly when he read the evening paper. He'd sit in his brown recliner—the one with the little tear on the right armrest where the cat scratched it—and he'd just mutter about the headlines. That cat, oh, she was a terror. A calico named Patches. We found her in a cardboard box behind the grocery store in the pouring rain. She was so tiny she fit right in the palm of my hand. I fed her warm milk with a little eye dropper for the first two weeks. She grew up to be the most demanding creature, always meowing for her supper right at five o'clock. If you were even a minute late, she'd weave between your legs until you nearly tripped over her. We had this yellow linoleum in the kitchen back then, and I was always terrified I'd slip and fall while holding her food dish... \n\nBut I don't know if someone telling me to move helps. I just haven't paid attention to that.\n\nDoctor: When you get frozen like that, do you ever feel compelled to hold that posture because someone or something is telling you to?\n\nPatient: Told to hold still? Oh, no, I don't hear anything telling me to do that. It's not an instruction or a voice or anything like that. It's funny you mention being told what to do, though. My grandmother used to give the most specific instructions when she was baking. She wouldn't let anyone else in the kitchen when she was making her apple pies. She said we'd ruin the temperature of the room and the crust wouldn't be flaky. She used this enormous wooden rolling pin that had belonged to her mother, and she'd roll the dough out on a piece of wax paper dusted with flour. She always used Granny Smith apples, said the tartness was the secret. She'd peel them so carefully, trying to get the whole peel off in one long green ribbon. My sister and I used to take the apple peels and throw them over our shoulders onto the floor. There was some old wives' tale that whatever letter the peel formed when it landed would be the first initial of the man you were going to marry. Mine always just looked like a squiggly line, or maybe an 'S' if you squinted. I ended up marrying a William, so I suppose the apple peel didn't know what it was talking about anyway... \n\nBut no, nobody is telling me to hold that posture. I just kind of get stuck, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have beliefs that other people thought were untrue or unusual?\n\nPatient: Untrue beliefs? No, I don't think anyone has ever told me that. I just believe what's normal, you know? Not like my old neighbor, Mrs. Gable—now she had some unusual beliefs. She swore up and down you had to plant your garden by the signs of the moon. She kept this worn-out Farmer's Almanac right on her kitchen counter, tucked behind a ceramic rooster that held all her wooden spoons. If the moon wasn't in the right phase, she wouldn't put a single seed in the dirt, even if it was a perfectly warm day in May and the ground was ready. She did grow the biggest beefsteak tomatoes, though, I'll give her that. We'd slice them thick and put them on soft white bread with just a little mayonnaise and a dash of salt. Oh, it was the best sandwich you could ever eat in the summertime. We used to sit out on her porch swing, eating our tomato sandwiches and watching the cars go by. There weren't nearly as many cars on the road back then. Mostly it was just Mr. Henderson driving his old blue pickup truck down to the feed store. He always had his golden retriever riding in the back, tongue just flapping in the wind... \n\nBut, no, nobody says my beliefs are untrue. I just mind my own business, really.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, if someone tries to move you, do you resist them?\n\nPatient: Resist them? I... I don't know. I don't think anyone has really tried to move me when I'm like that. Mostly people just leave me be. My sister used to be the one who wouldn't leave well enough alone, always trying to fix things that weren't broken. Like that time she tried to cut her own bangs right before our cousin's wedding. She used these big, heavy fabric shears that my mother kept hidden in a tin box under the sewing machine. Mother was furious, of course. She said those scissors were only for cutting dress patterns and now they'd be dull. My sister ended up with her hair chopped straight across, about an inch above her eyebrows. She looked like a startled owl in all the wedding photographs. It was a beautiful wedding, though. They had it in a little white church with stained glass windows that caught the afternoon sun just perfectly. They served a white cake with buttercream frosting that melted right on your tongue... \n\nBut no, I don't think anyone tries to push me or move my arms or anything. So I couldn't say if I resist.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Unusually sad? Oh, I don't think so. I mean, everybody gets a little down sometimes, but not for days and days. Not like my Uncle Arthur. Now, he was a sad sort of man. He used to sit in this big, green leather recliner in the corner of his living room, just staring out the window for hours. He had this little transistor radio he kept on the side table, always tuned to the baseball game, but I don't think he was really listening. He smoked a pipe, too—cherry tobacco. The whole house smelled like it. Sweet, but kind of heavy, you know? He had this old hound dog named Buster that would sleep right at his feet. Buster was mostly blind by the time I was old enough to remember him, and he had this funny way of walking where his back legs kind of went sideways. We used to try to get Buster to chase after a tennis ball, but he'd just look at us and lay his head back down on his paws... \n\nBut no, I don't get sad like that for days on end. I just keep busy, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices talking to you or about you when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, I don't hear any voices. It's usually pretty quiet around here. Too quiet sometimes, if you ask me. I used to love listening to the radio when I was a girl. We had this big wooden console radio right in the parlor, and on Sunday nights my father would tune it in to hear the mystery programs. The Shadow, that was his favorite. He'd sit in his rocking chair, the one with the woven cane seat that squeaked every time he leaned back. Squeak, squeak, squeak. My mother hated that noise. She tried oiling the runners once with some sewing machine oil, but it just made a mess on the braided rug. She made that rug herself, you know, from old wool coats and torn skirts. It took her the better part of a winter to finish it, sitting by the wood stove with her big wooden needle. She had hands that were always busy, mother did. Even when she was just sitting, she was knitting or darning socks... \n\nBut no, no voices talking to me. Just my own thoughts, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arm or hand while you’re frozen, does it stay in that position without much resistance?\n\nPatient: Stay in that position? Well... I'm not really sure about that. Like I said before, nobody really bothers me when I get like that. They just let me be. Which is nice, I suppose. People used to be a lot more hands-on, didn't they? I remember when I was a little girl, the school nurse, Mrs. Gable, she was always poking and prodding at us. She had these freezing cold hands. She'd check our hair for lice with these two wooden sticks, just parting the hair over and over. She smelled like peppermint and rubbing alcohol. She lived in this little blue house right at the end of Elm Street, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree must have been a hundred years old. In the autumn, the leaves would turn this brilliant, fiery red, and they'd cover the whole lawn. We used to rake them up into these massive piles and just jump right in. My brother lost his shoe in one of those piles once. It was a brand new Buster Brown shoe, too. Mother made him stay out there until it got dark looking for it, but he never found it... \n\nBut anyway, I couldn't tell you if my arm stays put. No one's ever tried it.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arm while you are frozen, does it stay in that position like wax for a while?\n\nPatient: Like wax? Well, I don't know about that. I just told you, nobody really comes around to move my arms when I'm stuck like that. Wax, though... that reminds me of my Aunt Clara. She used to make her own candles, you know. Beeswax. She had these big blocks of it she kept in the pantry, and she'd melt it down on the stove in this old, dented double boiler. The whole kitchen would smell like honey and smoke. She'd pour it into these little tin molds, and we had to be so careful not to bump the table while they were setting. She made a batch once for Christmas, dyed them bright red with some kind of powder she got from the apothecary. They burned so pretty, but the wax dripped all over the good lace tablecloth. Mother was furious. She tried freezing the cloth and scraping the wax off with a butter knife, but it left this pink stain right in the middle. We had to put a big bowl of pinecones over it every time we had company... \n\nBut no, nobody moves my arms, so I couldn't say if they stay like wax."
  }
